You must not be influenced in any way by the unpleasant nature  of some of the evidence, including testimony, including exhibits. Terri Austin ([03:10:12](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE2YjMwODZlN2E1NzUzYjBmNDcyMTllNkE1enR3WEVSelB0/o/VEMwMTcwODA3MDY1?ts=11412.18)):

Exactly,  and I think that's why the judge is giving them an early break. Those are the people who  saw Patrick Clancy before he ended up going home and finding his wife  on the ground and his three children, who obviously were killed. But I  do believe that there was a possibility to avoid these two witnesses  altogether. You saw that Reddington did not cross-examine them. Both of  them were short, they could have stipulated to this. If I'm sitting on  that jury, I'm thinking, why are you wasting my time, prosecution,  listening to what he ordered? I know she's trying to show that the  defendant, Lindsay Clancy, knew what she was doing, it was planned, and  it was organized.

([03:11:04](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE2YjMwODZlN2E1NzUzYjBmNDcyMTllNkE1enR3WEVSelB0/o/VEMwMTcwODA3MDY1?ts=11464.8))
Frankly,  I think that's something that even the defense could stipulate if he  can get an expert to say you can be both psychotic and planned, and  those two things are not mutually exclusive. So, maybe he doesn't want  to admit that here, and maybe that's why he is allowing these people to  go on, and didn't offer to stipulate, but I probably would've stipulated  to that.

Julia Jenae ([03:11:26](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE2YjMwODZlN2E1NzUzYjBmNDcyMTllNkE1enR3WEVSelB0/o/VEMwMTcwODA3MDY1?ts=11486.58)):

Well,  let's talk about that, Terri, a little bit more, because actually, the  defense attorney has tried to stipulate to things in this case and it  has been rejected by the court or the prosecution. They didn't want the clothing to come in, the autopsy photos,  the crime scene photos, the first responders who are going to be  testifying to exactly what happened, because he has said during pretrial  motions, "I would stipulate to all of this. We don't even have to have a  guilt phase. She's admitting guilt. She's not admitting that she was  not insane at the time, and we could just have a phase that focuses on  her mental health. Was this psychosis something where she didn't  understand right from wrong?" Do you think that that would've been the  way to go, that the court should have agreed with him, but clearly, that  didn't happen?

Terri Austin ([03:12:16](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE2YjMwODZlN2E1NzUzYjBmNDcyMTllNkE1enR3WEVSelB0/o/VEMwMTcwODA3MDY1?ts=11536.41)):

I  think the prosecution has a right to make the argument. ([03:12:58](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE2YjMwODZlN2E1NzUzYjBmNDcyMTllNkE1enR3WEVSelB0/o/VEMwMTcwODA3MDY1?ts=11578.53))
But  she's got to put on her case. The judge is giving her leeway. And I  think the defense, Reddington, is an amazing defense attorney. I think  we've all been saying that, and I think he made the argument and the  judge basically was not having it.

Julia Jenae ([03:13:12](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE2YjMwODZlN2E1NzUzYjBmNDcyMTllNkE1enR3WEVSelB0/o/VEMwMTcwODA3MDY1?ts=11592.15)):

Terri Austin ([03:13:46](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE2YjMwODZlN2E1NzUzYjBmNDcyMTllNkE1enR3WEVSelB0/o/VEMwMTcwODA3MDY1?ts=11626.38)):

Overkill,  in my opinion, going to all three locations. Maybe going to the home.  You have photos, you have 3D representations, and you heard Reddington  argue, "You know, you're going to get to see all this and you're going  to go to the scene." I would have, if I were the judge, said, "Yes, you  can go to the house," but I don't think they need to go to the CVS. For  what purpose? The defendant had nothing to do with the CVS. Patrick  walked to the CVS. What are we going to see, where he walked and picked  up the drugs? What difference does that make? And why are we going to  the ThreeV? He picked up the food. There's no doubt that he picked up  the food. Complete total waste of time. It's hard to get a jury to get  on a bus, or however they're being transported, to make sure everybody  gets out, to make sure that they all see the same thing, and to make  sure no one goes off and sees something else.

([03:14:32](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE2YjMwODZlN2E1NzUzYjBmNDcyMTllNkE1enR3WEVSelB0/o/VEMwMTcwODA3MDY1?ts=11672.76))
I  mean, going to a scene is not the easiest thing in the world. I  would've limited, had I been the judge, to just the home. The judge is being very careful, and he is  allowing the prosecution to go forward and to prove their case, so  that's why we're seeing three things.

Julia Jenae ([03:14:52](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE2YjMwODZlN2E1NzUzYjBmNDcyMTllNkE1enR3WEVSelB0/o/VEMwMTcwODA3MDY1?ts=11692.32)):

No,  I agree. The logistics of a jury view, not easy, not simple. It is an  undertaking to get all of these different people. I don't know if they're going to go into  the ThreeV and into the CVS, but the prosecution says they want them to  feel the distance, feel the amount of time that it took to get to these  locations. I mean, this is probably why we have this six to eight-week  trial, Terri, because we can already tell at the onset, the  meticulousness and thoroughness that this prosecution is going to be  taking in going through the facts of this case.

Terri Austin ([03:15:36](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE2YjMwODZlN2E1NzUzYjBmNDcyMTllNkE1enR3WEVSelB0/o/VEMwMTcwODA3MDY1?ts=11736.06)):

Welcome  back to Court TV Live, I'm Julia Jenae. And we are continuing our  coverage out of Plymouth, Massachusetts, in the case against Lindsay  Clancy, a mom on trial for killing her three kids. She does not deny  that she did that, but her defense says she was suffering from  postpartum psychosis at the time and should not be held criminally  responsible. The first witness for the prosecution was one who broke all  of our hearts, Patrick Clancy, the father and former husband of the  defendant. And I want to bring in someone who was inside of the  courtroom for the most emotional of moments that happened today. We have  Director of Photography for Law and Crime and Court TV, Richie  Morganson is with us, Morganstern rather, there in Boston,  Massachusetts.

([03:21:58](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE2YjMwODZlN2E1NzUzYjBmNDcyMTllNkE1enR3WEVSelB0/o/VEMwMTcwODA3MDY1?ts=12118.23))
Richie,  thank you so much for joining us. I know you're on a lunch break at  this time, so we certainly appreciate your time. Yes.  Of course, after hearing it, I think it was a great idea that the  public did not have to hear this. A lot of us have been doing this for a  while, but today was something else. Obviously,  Lindsay was breaking down. It was quite the show for emotion at that  point.

... at her?

Duxbury,  Massachusetts, a quiet seaside town was shattered by an unthinkable  tragedy. Lindsay Clancy, a young nurse, devoted wife, and mother of  three, was seen as the picture of perfection. But police say behind the  smiles in family photos, darkness took hold. On a cold January night,  investigators say she strangled her children, Cora, Dawson, and baby  Callan. All that before attempting to take her own life. Clancy's  husband, Patrick, returned home to a scene of unimaginable horror. Now,  the once trusted nurse faces murder charges as her defense claims she  suffered from severe postpartum psychosis. Prosecutors call it  calculated. Her attorneys say it's a symptom of a mind lost to illness.

We are continuing to  wait for court to start up again, so let's bring in our panel of guests  to talk about where we are in this case and whether or not the  prosecution is going to be successful, especially in a claim that there  was some malingering or faking by the defendant.

I don't want to disparage or downgrade  the duties and obligations and job description of people who work in a  CVS pharmacy, but make no mistake about it, they are not trained in  recognizing psychosis, hallucinations, delusional disorder. They're  there just to ring up sales and reconcile the books at the end of the  day and make sure the shelves are stocked.

([04:06:35](https://www.rev.com/app/transcript/NmE2YjMwODZlN2E1NzUzYjBmNDcyMTllNkE1enR3WEVSelB0/o/VEMwMTcwODA3MDY1?ts=14795.46))

So for example, "Did you ever have anybody in line  vomiting that needed medication?" And then the jury's going to be looking at the prosecutor  asking themselves why do they even call these people to the stand?
